2026-10-04T19:02:06Z <codex:23-34-49-01a0f587-6fab-7313-a2e0-9a7ea1b22cd1> AGENT: **Equal prefix state is insufficient to skip a suffix across these code versions.** Later changed consumers and completed-history facts still need checking.
    
    The smallest sound implementation is:
    
    1. **Extend the inventory to all occurrences** of changed decisions. Keep exact prefixes and original mask unions; discard later checks only where an earlier replacement already covers their masks.
    2. **Probe one native transition per distinct prefix.** Reuse `capture_many`’s callback wrappers; stop each child at its first appended decision or terminal. Compare saved versus native actions, masks, answer domains, edge expressions, full `_S`, and continuation identity.
    3. **Retain matching children; replace only divergent continuations.** A matching transition permits advancing to the next relevant check—not certifying every descendant.
    4. **Certify financial reuse separately.** Compare corrected saved-history `event_trace` through `_Walk.equivalence` against preserved raw equivalence keys on identical masks. Refresh completed question facts through `question_refresh`.
    5. **Reject cuts inside watches.** Returning early there can make `_watched` report false and omit `_edge_after`’s ancestor probability. Promote to the watch owner; do not reuse its old read flag automatically.
    
    Use `notes_topology.identity` for callback/closure comparisons. Do not use the old `notes_scope` root flags or cold old-engine replay as financial certificates: the demonstrated sizing defect depended on intermediate row populations.
    
    This avoids blanket motion-family replacement and full-tree enumeration, while retaining explicit checks for later changed decisions.

    THE GOAL
    
    This is the work sample for Russell, now about ten days past the call. Russell opens one page. It traces a real filing through Jev's judgment and the cash mechanism to Slope's dated collections on Akoustis's existing line, reviewed 14 May 2024 with a horizon of 10 Nov. The page separates the lawsuit's effect from ordinary operating risk on the same 512 operating draws. Russell can move a judgment and watch the cash move. Code owns every date, amount and probability; Jev judges one well-defined uncertainty per question, using facts as they stood before the answer. The page makes no recommendation. Since your 1 Oct reframe, the system itself is the point: one repeatable `slope analyze-case` run (agent → Jev → engine → page). The Akoustis tree matters only as its demonstration.
    
    WHERE WE ARE
    
    - Code: PR #21 (step-9-memo) is at 1ca49d1 and pushed.
    - About 815 lines of fixes and 4 new test files sit uncommitted in that worktree. This is Codex's last correction and the most fragile thing we have; it exists only on disk.
    - What the correction does:
      - A decision is scheduled by its own date, not by the furthest date the walk happened to read.
      - Where two decisions fall in a different order on different draws, the draws are split by their actual order. Before, the order on one draw was applied to all of them.
      - The court question sees the same pre-court facts and the same settlement offer whether the stay is granted or denied.
      - A question that no longer applies keeps its own answer choices.
      - A stay no longer reserves its own collateral while it is being sized.
    - How far it's proven: on the two failing groups of histories, on their original draws (374 and 289), probability sums to 1 on every draw. That holds using only the questions Jev would actually see, with no missing answers. 42 focused tests pass.
    - Production data: the 2 Oct walk and everything built on it are in S3 (6.56M histories → 2.20M paths; the pooled set has 21,878 live questions). That set can't be used. The overall probability check failed by up to 0.8% on one draw, and the 100-question read failed. Both trace to the defects the correction fixes.
    - Codex stopped partway through working out which saved histories survive the correction. That list doesn't exist yet.
    - Jev has never run on this tree and stays on hold until you say start. It's about 20k questions, roughly $14 at the configured rate.
    - The Jev → financial reduction → page stages have never run on production data.
    - AWS: i-00b6051aa298e85e2 (m6a.4xlarge, about $0.69/h) has been running idle since 18:40 UTC. There's no shutdown timer, and its 1 TB disk is kept.
    - CI is not green. The assumptions test fails because the variant runs haven't been re-recorded, and the full-tree test in test_pending_claim times out. PR #20 (spec text) is open. PR #22 (Rust) is out of scope.
    
    What has blocked delivery for five days is one class of defect. The order in which the walk visited decisions stood in for the engine's dated, before-answer state. Each overall check exposed another instance. Each fix changed question identities, which forced a choice between repairing saved work and walking again. Codex's last correction fixes the class at its source, where dates set both the order and the facts. The financial mechanics and the compute fleets work.
    
    THE ONE OPEN DECISION: WALK AGAIN OR REPAIR SAVED WORK
    
    My lean is a fresh walk on the corrected code. In the main affected group, only 448 of 1,846 saved histories fully match the corrected walk; the rest are reordered, changed or new. Matching those up by question identity is exactly where the repeated defects came from, such as one question being swapped in for another with a different set of answers.
    
    A fresh walk records the correct facts as it goes, so the question-repair stage disappears. Pooling took about 2.5 hours last time. The 2 Oct walk took about 15 hours, almost all of it waiting on a few very large subtrees. Those can now be split finely from the start.
    
    What would change my lean is the corrected walk growing the tree a lot (that affected group went from 1,846 to 2,941 histories). The walk's first hour on the heaviest parts shows that directly, without a separate measuring run.
    
    How I'll work:
    - Reason from the code; no comparison harnesses.
    - Finish each fix in the same turn.
    - Watch progress without being asked.
    - GitHub first, AWS only to speed things up.
    - Never an automatic shutdown; nothing heavy on the Mac.
    - Plain words.
    - Jev stays held until you say start.
    
    NEXT FIVE
    
    1. Commit and push the uncommitted correction to step-9-memo. Why: it's two days of work that exists only on this disk.
    2. Stop the idle AWS host (stop, not terminate; disk kept) until the walk needs it. Why: it bills for nothing, and a deliberate stop is not the automatic shutdown you ruled out.
    3. Launch the fresh walk on the corrected code: GitHub first (80 jobs, every core), AWS on the heaviest parts, fine splitting from the start. Why: the old saved tree carries the defect, and a fresh walk avoids repair work that kept introducing new errors.
    4. Pool the results, run the overall probability check, read 100 actual rendered questions, then come back to you ready for Jev. Why: those two checks caught every defect so far.
    5. On your start: run Jev, the financial reduction and the page, then read the rendered page myself and draft the note to Russell. Why: that page is what Russell opens.
    
    Telos: Russell follows one real filing into Jev's judgment and sees it move Slope's dated collections on Akoustis's line, separate from ordinary operating risk. Every number traces to dated evidence, a judgment or a rule.
    
    The design is settled. What's left is to finish once on corrected code rather than keep repairing saved work. Say GO for steps 1–3, or tell me if you'd rather repair the saved work.

2026-10-04T20:11:48Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798065] AGENT: Steps 1–3 are done and the walk is running. To answer your question directly: the split we used on Oct 2 was not optimized. I measured it from its own logs and rebuilt it before launching.
    
    WHY OCT 2 TOOK 15.5 HOURS
    - The walk itself was about 3.7 hours of work if spread evenly. Each path takes about 0.7 seconds per process.
    - Every task first re-walked the whole shared top of the tree. That cost 9–14 minutes per task, before any of its own work.
    - The tree was cut into 100 groups of four tasks each, no matter how big the branches inside were. That produced tasks of 3 to 5.6 hours, and the heaviest weren't started first.
    - The slow tasks were re-split three times. Each re-split threw away their partial progress and paid the setup again.
    
    WHAT THE NEW SPLIT DOES
    1. A shorter shared top. The corrected tree has a much bigger top: at the old cut depth of 10, it has 20,838 branches and takes 22 minutes, repeated by every task. At cut depth 6 it takes 23 seconds. Each task then walks only the upper levels of its own branches. The cut depth only decides where shared work ends and task work begins; the results are identical either way.
    2. Costs from Oct 2's saved paths. The corrected walk orders decisions by date, so I matched Oct 2's 6.57M saved paths to the new branches by the set of decisions they contain, not their order. All but 13 matched.
    3. Depth per branch from measured cost. For each branch I measured its largest piece and the cost of its upper levels at every depth. Upper levels cost 0.074 s per branch point, calibrated against the 22-minute top. Each branch is split at the shallowest depth where its largest piece fits one task. If none fits, it gets the depth with the shortest worst-case task.
    4. Heavy branches get their own groups. That way their tasks re-walk only their own upper levels. Task size grows with those upper levels, so that walking them stays about 15% of a task. Light branches are packed together.
    5. Longest tasks first. All workers claim from one shared queue, longest estimated task first. GitHub tasks are claimed only while their AWS credential has two hours left. The AWS host renews its own credential, and an assembler folds each group as soon as all of its tasks are saved.
    
    The result is 1,050 tasks with a median of 50 minutes. At Oct 2's tree size that is 1,425 process-hours, about 19% of it the repeated upper-level walking. The longest task is 3.25 hours, and it started first.
    
    WHERE IT STANDS (20:12 UTC)
    - All 176 processes are walking: 40 GitHub jobs × 4, plus 16 on AWS.
    - Commits 2991d27 (the correction) through e9e166d and later are pushed to PR #21.
    - Your viewer at http://127.0.0.1:18766 shows the walk: 1,050 tasks, 176 active.
    
    Capacity is the limit. GitHub now runs only 40 jobs at once (the 80 seen on Oct 2 isn't available today). So instead of 4.2 hours on 336 processes, it's about 8 hours on 176, more if the corrected tree is bigger than Oct 2's. I've asked AWS to raise the 16-vCPU limit to 128. That became a support case and isn't approved yet.
    
    NEXT FIVE
    1. In about 25 minutes, check the real speed once the heavy groups finish their upper levels and start producing paths. Why: it shows whether the corrected tree is bigger or slower than Oct 2's, which sets the true finish time.
    2. Before 23:30 UTC, renew the GitHub credential. Why: GitHub stops claiming at 23:56, and the 80 jobs waiting in the queue read the new credential when they start, so work continues without a gap.
    3. If AWS approves the quota, launch an extra on-demand host on the same queue (about $0.04 per vCPU-hour). Why: it's the only way to add capacity beyond GitHub's 40 jobs.
    4. As groups finish, check that each assembled group covers all of its branches, then pool. Why: pooling can start the moment the 100th group is saved.
    5. Run the overall probability check, read 100 actual questions, and come back to you ready for Jev. Why: those two checks caught every defect so far.
    
    Telos: Russell follows one real filing into Jev's judgment and sees it move Slope's dated collections on Akoustis's line, separate from ordinary operating risk.
    
    The split now puts work where it costs; what remains is capacity and making sure work continues across the credential change.

2026-10-04T20:46:17Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798097] AGENT: What "13 didn't match" means. To schedule the walk, I estimated each new branch's cost by counting Oct 2's saved paths that contain all of that branch's decisions. Of Oct 2's 6,573,684 saved paths, 13 contained the full decision set of no branch in the corrected tree, so they weren't counted toward any estimate.
    
    That affects only the scheduling estimate, not the walk. The walk covers every branch of the corrected tree, and assembly checks that coverage against the tree's own list of branches. It never consults Oct 2.
    
    I don't yet know what those 13 histories are. My first guess, that they end before the cut depth, is wrong: the skeleton has none. Most likely they are histories whose decisions the corrected engine labels or orders differently, so no new branch's set fits. The scan that lists them is running at low priority on the host and prints when it finishes; I'll bring you the 13 then.
    
    Status at 20:45 UTC, 46 minutes in:
    
    - All 176 processes are walking: 40 GitHub jobs × 4, plus 16 on AWS. The other 40 GitHub jobs are queued; the account runs 40 at once.
    - 283,000 paths have been walked. No task has finished yet; the median task should end in about 20–30 minutes.
    - The heavy groups are working: groups 0–3 have produced 32,000–53,000 paths each. Their upper levels took 13–14 minutes per task, not the 25 I estimated.
    - The AWS quota increase is still a support case.
    
    One finding changes the timeline. Throughput is about 105 paths per second across the fleet, and the median process does 0.66 paths per second. Oct 2 did 1.46 on the same kind of work, so the corrected walk costs about twice as much per path.
    
    At this rate the walk takes about 17 hours if the tree is Oct 2's size, and longer if the tree is larger. Part of the early rate is upper-level time, so it will rise somewhat, but not twofold.
    
    The likely cause is the work the correction added at every decision: capturing full before-answer facts, and splitting draws by decision order. The current tasks keep running, so nothing is lost.

2026-10-04T20:52:57Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798129] AGENT: Yes. Codex's last round added a substantial per-path cost to the walk. The profile and the code agree on where it comes from.
    
    The measurement: on the corrected code, the median walker produces 0.66 paths per second. Oct 2 managed 1.46. I sampled two running walkers on the AWS host for 60 seconds each. Almost all their time (88–95%) goes into building traces, and inside those traces most of it is new or newly invoked work:
    
    1. Before-answer fact capture (65aec3a). Every trace that isn't a light structural read now records full question facts at every decision on the path. That is needed where a question's facts are recorded, but it was switched on for every such trace.
    2. `court_questions` (2991d27). For every stay on a traced path, it builds a fresh chain and replays the whole path from the start, to read the court question before approval. Its cache is keyed on the full history, which is unique to nearly every path, so it almost never hits. It takes 25–27% of samples.
    3. `moves_cash`, the test that decides whether two branches merge. It only compares a cash fingerprint, but it asks for those full traces, so it pays for both items above. On one walker it took 54% of the time.
    
    The fix (f641cc1, pushed): `moves_cash` now compares light traces. A light trace keeps the event cash whole, and the existing shadow check `light_trace` asserts that its cash equals the full trace's, so every merge decision is unchanged. 49 focused tests pass. The AWS host's checkout is already on it, so each task it starts from now on uses it.
    
    What the fix leaves:
    
    - Captures and court rereads still run where facts are actually recorded (`_facts`, and `emit` at path end). There they are part of what the correction requires.
    - I expect the fix to recover a large share of the loss, not all of it. The first AWS tasks on the new code will show by how much.
    
    The GitHub decision. The 40 running GitHub jobs and the 80 queued ones are pinned to the old code (2780115). If they keep going, they claim tasks at half speed until the credential guard stops them around 23:56 UTC. If I cancel them, the 160 in-flight tasks lose about 50 minutes each, about 4% of the total work at the new speed. Running old code for three more hours wastes several times that.
    
    My lean is to cancel both GitHub runs as soon as the first AWS task on f641cc1 confirms the speedup, about 20–30 minutes from now. Then I relaunch 40 jobs on f641cc1 with a fresh credential. Their tasks restart from the shared queue, so nothing completed is lost. Unless you say otherwise, I'll do that.

2026-10-04T22:15:51Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798169] AGENT: Status at 22:15 UTC:
    
    - The fix is confirmed. Workers on f641cc1 walk at a median of 1.88 paths per second per process. The pre-fix corrected code managed 0.66, and Oct 2 managed 1.46. So the corrected walk now runs faster per path than Oct 2.
    - I restarted GitHub on 7fb2e19 (run 37238943603) to fix a scheduling defect that my first cancellation exposed. Workers passed once down the task list and skipped claimed tasks until the end of the pass. That left about 150 abandoned heavy tasks to start last, which would have added 1–2.5 hours of tail. Now each core re-reads the queue after every task and takes the highest-priority open task, so abandoned or failed heavy tasks are retaken first. The restart cost about 37 minutes of fleet time against that 1–2.5 hour tail.

2026-10-04T22:20:56Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798194] AGENT: I've checked those logs. The errors aren't walk failures. They come from a race in the claim code I wrote, and I've fixed it (ca6ff28, pushed).
    
    **What the lines mean.** When a task's claim has gone stale (no heartbeat for five minutes), any core may take it over: it reads the claim, then deletes it. After the restart, every core on all 40 jobs went for the same few abandoned heavy tasks at once. One core won each race. For the others, the claim had already been deleted or replaced between their read and their delete. That surfaced as `NoSuchKey` or `ConditionalRequestConflict`, which the worker logged as "partition retry required". Each of those lines is a core that lost the race and moved on. The `start subdivision` lines in between are its next claims, which went through.
    
    **The real harm was small but worth fixing.** Each lost race counted as a failure against that task. After two failures, a job stops trying the task. Today that does nothing, because every contested task has a live owner. But if one of those owners later hits the separate fault (more below), many jobs would refuse to pick the task back up.
    
    **The fix:**
    - A lost race now returns quietly.
    - Only a walk that actually exits with an error counts against a task.
    - The log line now names the task.
    
    **I'm not restarting GitHub for this.** A restart would throw away about 25 minutes of work on 156 processes, and the defect costs nothing right now. The fix goes in with the next relaunch. That relaunch is needed anyway: either to fix the separate fault where heavy tasks exit with an error, or to renew the credential before about 01:10 UTC.
    
    **That separate fault is still open.** No failed-task log has arrived yet in `refine-v1/failed/`. The standalone rerun of task 0-0 on the AWS host has passed 1,000 paths without failing. The earlier failures came at about 1,600 seconds, so its result is due soon.
    
    **Status, 22:20 UTC:**
    - 172 tasks are walking: 156 on GitHub, 16 on AWS.
    - 607,000 paths walked; no task saved yet.
    - No failed attempts on the new code so far.
    
    **The new viewer is up** at http://127.0.0.1:18766. It shows every step from investigation to the analysis page, each with its state and progress bar. It also has a 100-square grid of the groups, a recent-events feed, and a failed-attempts list with links to each failed log. Two caveats:
    - Its rate and finish estimate (90 paths a second, finishing Monday 16:45) are wrong for now. The viewer only started a few minutes ago, so its 15-minute window isn't full yet. On the measured 1.88 paths per second per process, the rate should settle near 320 a second.
    - The later steps (pooling through the page) show "waiting" until each step reports its progress.

2026-10-04T22:25:24Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798201] AGENT: That's by design, and it's the right order. All 172 processes are busy; they're just all working inside the heaviest groups.
    
    **Why 9 groups**
    
    - Every core takes the open task with the longest estimated run time. Groups 0–15 hold the heaviest branches, each in a group of its own, cut into 19–48 tasks.
    - Groups 0–7 alone have 198 tasks, more than GitHub's 156 processes. So GitHub fills groups 0–7.
    - The AWS host's 16 processes are in group 0 and group 13. Its worker began before my scheduling fix and moved on to group 13 tasks after group 0's failures.
    - That makes 9 groups.
    
    **Why that's right**
    
    - How many groups are walking doesn't change speed. What counts is that every core is busy and the longest tasks start first.
    - Spreading cores across all 100 groups would leave the 3-hour tasks for last. That is the tail that cost Oct 2 hours of wall time.
    - As the heavy tasks finish, cores move down the list to lighter groups. The light groups' short tasks fill in at the end.
    - One side effect: no group assembles until late in the walk. That costs nothing, because pooling needs all 100 groups anyway.
    
    **The real risk in this order** is the open fault: heavy tasks in groups 0 and 1 exiting with an error. With cores concentrated there, a fault in heavy tasks would hit many cores at once. The standalone rerun of task 0-0 on the host, and the `refine-v1/failed/` logs, will show the cause. Both are due within about 20 minutes. If it's a real engine error, I fix it and relaunch with the claim-race fix in the same push.
    
    One correction: my live per-group claim count just failed (a date-parsing bug in the shell script), so the 9 above comes from the plan's task counts and the host's own log, not a fresh listing. The grid in the viewer shows it live: blue outlines mark the groups with tasks walking now.

2026-10-04T23:16:07Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798289] AGENT: The traceback for the failures showed two engine defects, and while checking them I found a third, more serious one. All three are fixed, and the walk restarted at 23:12 UTC on d3607c1.
    
    **The three defects**
    1. **The holders' delisting decision was asked twice on some paths** (7 of the 11 failed attempts). Codex's ordering correction lets a nested chain resolve that decision early, at its own date. The outer branch then asked it again, so the path recorded two of them, and the step that saves the question's facts raised an error. The decision is now asked once.
    2. **An empty cash-engine checkpoint** (4 of 11). On a group of draws that never reaches the checkpoint day, the cash engine tried to cache nothing and crashed. It now skips the cache there, which affects only speed.
    3. **A made-up petition date.** I found this while checking two failing tests on the older 20 June tree. Codex's levy-queue change from earlier today (1ca49d1) left a pre-ruling levy unbooked whenever every later step on a draw fell after the horizon. The company's post-ruling response then took the pre-ruling levy's date. On a "file" answer, that put a bankruptcy petition on day 60 for a response that doesn't occur inside the horizon at all. That is a fabricated payment date, one of the critical errors in CLAUDE.md, on 128 of 432 filing paths in the 20 June tree. The 14 May walk runs the same code, so the walk that was running would have carried it.
    
    **Why I restarted.** Defect 3 changes results on every path it touches, and no task had saved output yet, so the restart lost only work in progress. 75 focused tests pass, and the 20 June filing test passes again. One remaining `test_chains` failure is a stale test: it reads the levy queue as a single levy and expects the old way of reading court facts. I'll update it in the PR.

2026-10-04T23:23:54Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798303] AGENT: Yes, it should be much faster. 150 a second is a startup effect, not the speed the walk will run at.
    
    **What the 288 processes are doing.** At 23:23, eleven minutes after the restart, every running task is in groups 0–11. Those are the heaviest branches, and the queue starts them first on purpose. Before producing its first path, each of their tasks walks its branch's upper levels, and the plan puts that at 18–26 minutes per task (median 18). So 231 of the 288 tasks have yet to report a path. The cores are fully busy, with a load of 64 on the 64-vCPU host; they're just spending this window on the upper levels.
    
    **What the walk will run at.** Tasks past their upper levels produce 2.63 paths per second each. Once the heavy tasks are through theirs, the fleet should reach about 750 paths a second while walking, or around 540 a second averaged over each task including its upper levels. The viewer's figure also lags early on. Each task reports only every 1,000 paths, and its 15-minute rate window still includes the start.
    
    **Finish estimate.** If the corrected tree is the same size as 2 October's (6.57M paths), the work is about 960 process-hours: about 700 walking paths and about 270 walking upper levels. On 288 processes that's about 3.3 hours, so finishing around 02:30–03:00 UTC. A bigger tree would push that later. The rate should climb from about 23:35 as the heavy tasks finish their upper levels. I'll check it at about 23:45 and give you a firmer time.
    
    **One real inefficiency.** About 270 of those process-hours, close to an hour of the whole fleet's time, go on re-walking the same upper levels. Every task in a heavy group walks its branch's upper levels again before reaching its own share. Splitting the work so each task inherits that walk instead of repeating it would save most of that hour. But it means changing how the walk is split and restarting it, which would cost more than it saves at this point. So I'm leaving this walk running and noting the fix for the next run.

    - I estimated 750 from the speed of each task's first 1,000 paths, about 2.6 a second. Those come from the shallow end of each branch.

    - The full trace taken at the end of each path, to record its question facts, takes 20–34% of the time.
    - The walk's own structural reads take 28–39%.
    - The cash engine's daily loop takes about 13%.
    
    That is the walk's ordinary cost, spread out. No one-line fix remains, unlike `moves_cash` earlier, and any deeper code change would mean restarting the walk, which costs more than it would save tonight.
    
    **The only lever left is more compute, and it doesn't pay off tonight.** GitHub admits 40 jobs at a time. AWS is at its new 128-vCPU limit, and the last increase took about two hours to approve, so another request would only arrive near the end of the walk. 2026-10-05T02:48:19Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798389] AGENT: Status at 02:45 UTC. The walk is nearly done, but none of the 100 groups has assembled. Every finished group fails the coverage check, and I'm tracing why.
    
    **The walk**
    - 413 of 1,050 tasks are saved. 8.56M of about 10.1M paths are walked, at about 1,000 paths a second. No task has failed.
    - GitHub is now running all 80 jobs at once, up from 40. With AWS that's 398 processes, so the walk should finish around 03:15 UTC.
    - The GitHub credential renews itself and is good until 07:25 UTC.
    
    **What's wrong with assembly**
    - 8 groups have every task saved, but the assembler rejects each one with "subdivision topology differs". The rejected groups so far are 4, 5, 6, 8, 9, 16, 17 and 22, all heavy groups split inside a branch.
    - In group 4, all 34 tasks walked the same 655-or-so pieces each. They agree on the branch's layout for the first ~7,100–7,500 pieces, then drift apart: a 2-event shift in numbering, and 1–3 extra pieces in some tasks.
    - Every task in a group walks the shared upper levels of its branch, then numbers the pieces below and keeps every 34th. That only works if every task sees exactly the same upper levels. These tasks don't: what a task walked earlier changes what it sees later. So once the numbering drifts, some pieces may be walked twice and others not at all. The check is right to refuse.
    - Each saved path is still a valid history. What's broken is coverage across tasks, so these groups can't be pooled as they stand.
    
    **Likely cause, not yet confirmed.** The 2 October walk used the same splitting and passed this check, so something added since then makes the walk depend on its own history. My main suspect is a cache that Codex's correction added for reading court questions. Its key leaves out which draws are being read, so a later read on a different set of draws can reuse an earlier result. That would change question classes, and through them how later decisions are grouped. My `moves_cash` change is a lesser suspect.
    
    **What's running now**
    - On the large AWS host I'm re-walking two tasks of group 4 (partitions 0 and 4), logging every upper-level step. The first step where the two logs differ names the decision and the cause.
    - They match through the first 1,500 steps. The divergence sits around step 8,300, so I expect it within about 30–60 minutes.
    - The main walk keeps running in the meantime. Stopping it wouldn't help, and the light groups may assemble cleanly.

    **Cost I should have prevented.** I said I'd stop the two extra hosts myself once the queue emptied. I didn't, so they sat idle for about 11.5 hours, roughly $55. I've stopped both now; their disks are kept. The coordinator host is still up for the assembler. I also stopped the renewal loop.
    
    **What the diagnosis found.** The two side-by-side re-walks of group 4 finished. They match for 7,876 steps, then split at one place:
    - After an I3 settlement is declined, the walk reaches the listing decision and checks whether two of its outcomes lead to the same cash. The outcomes are "compliant" (the stock regains its required bid price) and "hearing" (it requests a hearing with Nasdaq).
    - Partition 0 treated them as different and walked both. Partition 4 treated them as the same and merged them.
    - The check (`_same_after`) compares cached chains and traces. So a cached result depends on what that process walked before.
    - From there the two tasks' numbering drifts apart, and each task keeps a different share of the pieces.
    
    **Why this matters beyond assembly.** If a merge decision depends on walk history, a single unsplit walk has the same problem. Two branches can be merged in one process and kept apart in another. If the merge is wrong, the merged branch takes the wrong cash. If the separation is wrong, it only adds paths. So I won't use the 66 assembled groups either until I know which answer was correct and why the cache gives two.
    
    **Next, in order**
    1. Replay that exact listing decision on its saved prefix twice, once in a fresh process and once after partition 4's history. Then compare the chain and trace fields `_same_after` reads. This is one bounded check, not a new walk, and it names the cache at fault.
    2. Fix the cache key, or the read that skips it, and add a test that the merge decision is the same in a fresh process and after prior walking.
    3. Decide what has to be walked again: the 34 failed groups at least, plus any assembled group whose merges the defect could have changed. Then rerun on GitHub (fresh credential) and AWS.
    4. Pool, run the overall probability check, and read 100 actual questions.
    5. Come back to you ready to run Clef. The judging still waits for your start.

    Since then I've done two things.
    
    **1. All spending is stopped.**
    - All three AWS hosts are stopped, with their disks kept.
    - The diagnostic walks and the group assembler on the coordinator are killed.
    - On your Mac, the credential-renewal loop and the status viewer are killed.
    - Nothing is running anywhere now.
    
    **2. I ran the one fast check on that listing decision, locally in about a minute:**
    - **The correct answer is "merge".** Computed from scratch with every cache empty, "compliant" and "hearing" lead to the same cash on all 56 draws of that path. Partition 4 merged them, which was right. Partition 0 kept them apart. That only adds duplicate paths; no branch got the wrong cash at this point.
    - **My stated cause was wrong.** I said the bug was the cache reusing a state built on a larger set of draws (97fbd72). Resuming from such a state gives a chain identical to the one built from scratch, field for field, and the same merge answer. The chain copies are clean too. So the history dependence comes from somewhere else, most likely the trace cache or the per-path draw sets the merge check reads. I don't know which yet, and I'm not going to guess a third time.
    
    **What that changes:**
    - The one disagreement we've traced was the harmless direction: a missed merge, which only adds paths. But I can't rule out the other direction elsewhere in the tree until I've found the cause.
    - So the 66 assembled groups are still unproven, and nothing gets pooled until the cause is fixed.
    
    **Next, one targeted run.** I'd run group 4's tasks once more with one change: at every merge decision, the walk also computes the answer from scratch. It stops at the first disagreement and saves both computations' state. That names the stale cache outright. Then I fix it, add a test that a merge decision is the same in a fresh process and after prior walking, and re-walk.
    - It runs on GitHub, which costs nothing.
    - Each task stops at its first disagreement and saves the state. The first one we traced came about 55 minutes into a task; computing each merge from scratch slows tasks down somewhat.
    - If you'd rather not spend that time, the alternative is to make the merge check always compute from scratch and re-walk directly. That's correct by construction, but slower to walk, by an amount I can't yet put a number on.
    
    My lean is the targeted run.
    
    Two decisions:
    1. Targeted run on GitHub, or the from-scratch merge check and re-walk directly?
    2. For the re-walk: GitHub only (free, 8–10 hours), or GitHub plus the 16-vCPU coordinator host (about $5)?

2026-10-05T16:05:26Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798496] AGENT: The targeted run is going, and I've worked through the cause from the code as far as it goes. It doesn't settle it yet, but it rules a lot out.
    
    **Running now:** GitHub run 37337750986, 9 jobs covering all 34 tasks of group 4, free.
    - Each task walks normally, and at every merge decision also works out the answer with every process cache empty.
    - At the first disagreement it records five things:
      - which stage of the comparison differed (the draws used, the day the branches diverge, the cash fingerprint);
      - every field where the cached chain and the cold chain differ, with the first differing value;
      - which single cache, emptied on its own, gives the cold answer;
      - the path;
      - then it stops.
    - I checked the instrumentation locally by forcing one disagreement, and the whole report path works.
    - In partition 0 the disagreement we know of came about 55 minutes into the walk; with the extra cold checks it will come later than that. Any other partition may hit one sooner, since the run catches disagreements that both original tasks happened to share.
    
    **Ruled out from the code:**
    - **The court-question cache:** it only feeds question facts, which the merge check never reads.
    - **Resuming from a cached state built on more draws:** tested directly, it gives an identical chain field for field and the same merge answer.
    - **Chain copies:** event arrays are copy-on-write, and the stack keeps copies, so finishing a trace can't touch them.
    - **The two engine caches that reuse earlier runs:** both are keyed by content and guarded against recycled objects.
    - **Sibling-trace reuse:** it's switched off (`REUSE = False`).
    
    **What the data says:**
    - The correct answer at the traced decision is "merge".
    - Partition 0 answered "different", so one of its comparisons saw a difference that a cold computation doesn't.
    - The part of the comparison that can produce that from history alone is the chain comparison (`divergence`). It compares every chain attribute it doesn't explicitly skip, and it doesn't skip the two cached-key attributes `_stay_owed` and `_price_key`. Those depend on what ran before. I haven't yet found the path where they end up different between two branches, so I'm not claiming them as the cause.
    - The run's field-by-field chain diff will confirm or rule them out directly.
    
    **When it reports:**
    1. Fix that exact state.
    2. Add a regression test for the same merge decision in a fresh process and after prior walking.
    3. Re-walk on GitHub plus the coordinator host (about $5, unless you say GitHub only). The worker stops its host once the queue is empty.
    
    I'll report the cause the moment the first task stops, and if one task reports a disagreement I'll stop the rest so they don't keep spending runner time.

2026-10-05T16:43:42Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798699] AGENT: I've re-read the governing documents, PLAN.md, the PR history and the build skill.
    
    What this is for
    - Russell, Slope's Head of Lending, told you about using sparse outside events, such as a lawsuit, that bank data can't see. He mentioned agent research with Jev. He also stressed that he values a loan by its own cash flows. He asked you to come back with a solution.
    - The thesis: domain-informed probabilistic judgments turn unusual qualitative evidence into a measurable financial signal.
    - The demo traces one chain on one page: evidence, then a judgment, then the cash mechanism, then dated collections on the loan. Russell can follow a real passage into a judgment, change it, and see the loan's cash flows move.
    - The no-lawsuit and with-lawsuit views share the same 512 operating draws, so the gap between them is the lawsuit's effect.
    - The demo makes no lending recommendation. The PLAN adds one more goal: a repeatable command (`slope analyze-case`) that runs agent, judgment model, engine and page, plus a second case, to show it isn't bespoke.
    
    How we got here
    1. Steps 1–8 (PRs #1–#19, merged to main) built the working system: dated evidence snapshots, the investigation agent, Jev judgments, Slope's reusable line and the loan's cash flows.
       - The Akoustis 14 May page is on main. It has 2,962 paths and 207 Jev answers.
       - With the lawsuit, collections by 10 Nov are 90.4% and the filing probability is 27.1%. Without it, 99.2% and 8.3%.
       - That page already shows the thesis end to end.
    2. Step 9 (from 28 Sep, PR #21, 286 commits beyond main) rewrote the 14 May questions to your stricter rules:
       - cash processed day by day;
       - each question carries the decision-maker's own record;
       - each draw is asked in its own situation class.
       That was a better model, but the tree went from 2,962 paths to about 6.5 million histories, now about 11 million, with about 21,900 live questions. From there the work became production computing: S3, GitHub fleets, AWS, and moving the AWS work from the company account to your personal one.
    3. 1–4 Oct: each check of that large tree exposed a defect, and each fix forced more recompute:
       - your 100-question reading found wording and timing errors;
       - the overall probability check failed (error 0.008);
       - Codex traced decision-order cycles and corrected them.
       PLAN.md itself warned on 1 Oct that the work was drifting into bespoke computing for one case. Its section 3 asked whether the problem really needs every history listed out, and that question was never answered.
    4. 4–5 Oct (me), in order:
       - started a fresh walk on the corrected code and fixed a slowdown (`moves_cash`);
       - fixed a race in claiming tasks, three engine faults and a pending-writ ordering bug;
       - swapped in Clef for every new judgment. It's tested live and held for your start.
       - The walk finished overnight, but 34 of 100 groups failed the coverage check. The cause is a merge decision that depends on what the process walked before. A wrong "merge" would give a branch the wrong cash, so none of the 66 assembled groups can be trusted yet either.
       - I also left two hosts idle for about 11.5 hours, about $55.
    
    Where things stand
    - Diagnostic run 37341813721 is running on GitHub at no cost. It will name the cache that flips the merge decision, and a watcher wakes me when a job stops.
    - Nothing is running on AWS.
    - Clef is held, and so are pooling and the page.
    - It's been about 10 days since the call with Russell.
    
    My read: the delay came from step 9's bigger tree. Once every history had to be listed out, each defect cost hours of compute and then exposed the next one. Merged main already demonstrates the thesis, but on the earlier, less rigorous questions.
    
    Next steps
    1. Name and fix the history-dependent merge as soon as the run reports, with a test that the decision is the same in a fresh process and after prior walking. Every downstream number depends on it.
    2. Decide the re-walk scope from that cause. A cause that can only produce false "different" answers just adds paths, so the 66 groups stand. One that can produce a false "same" means a full re-walk.
    3. Pool, run the overall probability check, then read 100 real rendered questions. These are the gates that failed before, so they decide whether Clef's answers mean anything.
    4. Run Clef on your start, then the financial reduction and the page. That is the deliverable Russell sees.
    5. Decide whether to keep paying for the full listing, or have Russell see the merged 14 May page now and follow with the step-9 version. Only you can weigh momentum against rigor.
    
    The aim isn't to finish this tree. It's to show Russell, credibly and soon, that researched evidence becomes a measurable, adjustable change in a loan's dated cash flows, through a pipeline that would work on the next case. Every remaining step should be judged by whether it gets that in front of him faster without making the numbers wrong.

2026-10-05T16:58:20Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798729] AGENT: Yes, it will report now, and I can see progress. Before this change I couldn't do either reliably, so I rebuilt the run and restarted it as run 37344049546. The first run was 16 minutes in, so that's all the restart lost.
    
    What was wrong:
    - GitHub only shows a job's log once the job ends. I checked: the log endpoint returns 404 while a job runs. So I had no view of progress, and the only signal was a job finishing.
    - The run might never have reproduced the bad merge. Its extra cold checks added entries to the engine's run cache, which changes what's held in memory as the walk goes. That's the kind of walk history that made the original task decide wrongly. The run could have walked for hours and found nothing.
    
    What it does now:
    - Two new jobs walk partitions 0 and 4 exactly as production did, with no extra checks until the merge we know went wrong. There they decide it with every cache empty, test each cache on its own to find which one flips the answer, and stop. Partition 0 hit that merge about 55 minutes in last time, so a report should come about then. Partition 4 is the control that merged correctly.
    - The other 9 jobs still check every merge decision in all 34 partitions, to catch any other disagreement.
    - Every job posts its progress to GitHub every 2 minutes as a status on the run's commit: minutes elapsed, then for each partition the paths walked and the merge checks passed. GitHub shows these while the job runs.
    
    How I'll know:
    - The watcher on your Mac (process 79195) reads those statuses every minute and logs them. It wakes me in either of two cases:
      - any job ends, with a report or cleanly;
      - no job's progress has changed for 15 minutes, once the run is 20 minutes old.
    - Checked at 16:58 UTC, after 4 minutes of walking: all 11 jobs are walking and posting. Seven partitions have passed 200–400 merge checks with no disagreement. Path counts start at 1,000, so they still read 0.
    
    Cost: nothing on AWS. The run uses GitHub runners, plus one GitHub API call a minute from your Mac.

2026-10-05T17:55:26Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798789] AGENT: The replica isn't running at half speed. I said that because I divided its path count by the minutes elapsed without looking at when it got there. The per-minute progress log shows it:
    
    - Partition 0 replica: 1,000 paths at 12 minutes, 2,000 at 18 minutes. Production: 1,000 at 10, 2,000 at 17. Same speed.
    - The partition 4 control: 1,000 at 10, 2,000 at 16, also normal.
    - Then both stopped adding paths at about 17:12 UTC and haven't moved for over 40 minutes. Partition 32, in the every-merge mode, stalled at 2,000 the same way.
    
    Most likely they reached the known merge earlier than I predicted and are inside the report. My 4,800-path estimate came from the merge's share of the task's steps, which doesn't map to paths. The report recomputes that one decision several times: from scratch with every cache emptied, once per cache, and twice more with full traces. It adds no paths while it works, so the count freezes. The every-merge jobs empty every cache at that same decision too, which would explain partition 32.
    
    What I don't know yet is whether they're computing or hung. GitHub hides live logs, so I'm measuring it directly:
    
    - On your Mac, one process (about 2 GB) is timing exactly what the report does at that merge, starting fresh. If that takes a few minutes, a 40-minute freeze means the replicas are stuck, not working. I'll find out why and say so plainly.
    - I also fixed the watcher. It counted the minute counter as progress, so its "no progress for 15 minutes" alarm would never have fired. The new watcher (process 86659) ignores the minutes.
    
    The timing comes back within about 10 minutes. I'll report it then, together with whether the replicas are computing or stuck.

2026-10-05T18:07:16Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798837] AGENT: It wasn't running at half speed. That was my misreading.
    
    The replicas ran at production speed: 2,000 paths at about 18 minutes, the same as production. Then they reached the bad merge at 20–25 minutes and wrote their reports. My harness hid that:
    - It ended the walk with exit code 3, but the walk runs inside a forked child process. The parent turned the 3 into an ordinary error and then hung instead of exiting.
    - So the job never stopped, and its status sat at 2,000 paths. Cancelling the run made each job upload its logs, and the reports were in them.
    
    **What the reports show (the cause)**
    - In partition 0, the walk judged "compliant" and "hearing" different. Computed from scratch, they are the same. Emptying only the cache of partly walked paths fixes it.
    - The cash in the two branches is identical: same fingerprint, same draws.
    - What differed was bookkeeping the comparison shouldn't read. When the walk serves a branch from that cache, the branch can carry levy entries copied in from a state built on more draws. On this path's 8 draws those entries are zero.
    - The merge comparison (`Chain.divergence`) counted two things as real differences:
      - those zero entries;
      - a stay cache key built from them (`_stay_owed`). It was missing from the list of fields the comparison skips, unlike its sibling `_price_key`.
    - So whether two branches merged depended on what that process happened to walk before. In partition 4 the leftover entries happened to match, so it merged correctly.
    - I suspected `_stay_owed` earlier and dropped it based on a test that only checked whether the key was set, not what it held.
    
    **What it did to results**
    - It could only make equal branches look different, never make different branches look equal. So the walk kept extra, redundant paths but booked no wrong cash.
    - The 66 assembled groups are valid. The 34 failed groups failed only because their tasks numbered pieces differently.
    
    **Fixed:** commit 3e14d97, pushed.
    - The comparison now ignores zero-amount entries and their order, and skips `_stay_owed`. A real levy still counts as a difference.
    - The new regression test passes, and so do 75 related engine tests. The one remaining failure (`test_each_court_ruling_on_a_motion_has_the_facts_of_its_own_day`) is the stale test PLAN.md already records, and it doesn't touch this code.
    
    **Running now**
    - The coordinator host is rebuilding the tree's top on the fixed code, about 15 minutes. If the 756 roots come out identical, only the 34 failed groups get re-walked and the 66 are kept. That's exactly what the plan comparison will show.
    - A watcher wakes me when it finishes, with the comparison.
    - Then I re-walk on GitHub (free), with assembly on the coordinator host (about $0.69 an hour). I stop the host myself the moment assembly finishes.

2026-10-05T18:31:58Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798876] USER: so explain what happened
2026-10-05T18:32:23Z <hermes:20261004_150251_ebc057> [798877] AGENT: What happened, start to finish:
    
    The defect
    - The walk keeps merging two branches when they lead to the same cash, to avoid walking duplicate futures. The check that decides this is `Chain.divergence`.
    - To save work, the walk serves a branch from a cache of partly walked paths. Sometimes that cached state was built on more draws than the path has, and gets cut down to the path's draws. Codex added that reuse after the 2 October walk.
    - A state cut down that way still carries levy entries from the other draws. On the path's own draws they are zero, so they book nothing, and the cash is identical either way.
    - The merge check still counted two of these leftovers as differences:
      - the zero-amount levy entries themselves;
      - a stay cache key built from them (`_stay_owed`). It was missing from the list of fields the check skips, while its sibling `_price_key` was on it.
    - So whether "compliant" and "hearing" merged depended on what that process had walked earlier. Partition 0 kept them apart; partition 4 merged them.
    - Each task in a group walks the shared top of the branch, then keeps every 34th piece. Once tasks disagreed on a merge, they numbered the pieces differently, so the assembly check rejected 34 groups.
    
    What it did and didn't do
    - It could only make equal branches look different, never the reverse. So it added redundant paths but booked no wrong cash.
    - The 66 groups that assembled are valid and kept. The 34 that failed are being re-walked now on the fix (3e14d97).
    
    How I handled it, and where I went wrong
    1. The walk finished at 02:57 EDT and nothing happened until you wrote at 11:30. I had nothing set to act when it ended. Two hosts sat idle for 11.5 hours, about $55.
    2. I guessed at the cause three times instead of reading the evidence:
       - First, the court-question cache. Wrong: the merge check never reads it.
       - Second, reuse of states built on more draws. That was the actual origin, but I tested it on a case with no levies on the other draws, saw no difference, and ruled it out.
       - Third, `_stay_owed`. That was the cause, but my test only checked whether the key was set, not what it held, and I dropped it.
    3. The diagnostic run went wrong three times:
       - The first version recomputed everything from scratch at every merge, which made it very slow. I restarted it.
       - The second gave no view of progress, because GitHub hides logs until a job ends. I restarted it with progress statuses.
       - The third worked. Both replicas reached the merge and wrote full reports 20–25 minutes in, around 17:15 UTC. But my report code exited the walk's forked worker process with code 3. The parent turned that into an ordinary error and then hung, so the job never stopped and its status froze at 2,000 paths.
    4. I told you the replica ran at half speed. I'd divided its paths by elapsed time without checking when it stopped moving. It had run at production speed until it reached the merge.
    5. I cancelled the run to force the logs out. The reports named the exact fields and the one cache that flipped the answer. The fix and its test followed within the half hour.
    
    What it cost
    - About 6.5 hours today from your 11:30 message to the re-walk starting (18:10 UTC), plus about $55 of idle hosts.
    - The answer was there by 17:15 UTC. My harness bug hid it for another 45 minutes.
    
    Where it stands
    - Re-walk of the 34 groups: GitHub plus the coordinator host, about 3–3.5 hours.
    - Then pooling, the overall probability check, and reading 100 real questions.
    - Then Clef, on your start.

    **Stop trigger**
    - Every 3 minutes a script checks each large host for walk processes. A host only runs one while it holds a task, and it re-reads the queue after every task.
    - When a host has no walk processes on two checks in a row, the script stops it: a stop, not a terminate, and the disk is kept. A busy host is never stopped.
    - The large hosts therefore stop as soon as no task is left for them to claim. They don't sit idle while GitHub and the coordinator finish the last few tasks.
    - The script wakes me when both are stopped, and logs each check to `stop_big.log`.
    
    **The coordinator host** stays up to assemble the 34 groups. The walk watcher wakes me when all 100 groups are assembled; I stop the coordinator then and go straight to pooling, the overall probability check and reading 100 real questions. Clef waits for your start.
